## Audio analysis source handoff and stash integration — 2026-10-20

Files now reads audio metadata in its existing guarded loading worker, instead of invoking the legacy synchronous Audio Studio constructor on the UI thread. The checked constructor installs its source context before scheduling analysis. Initial analysis, queue retries, explicit restarts, and both gain-only and spectral-filter edited-analysis workers validate before decoding and before each progressive publication. Failed validation cancels later stages, suppresses stale progress and preserves the source error. Earlier valid snapshots may remain visible. A shared streaming-analysis adapter handles both source and edited analysis; `ReadCheck::validate` shares the existing callback protocol without requiring an artificial read closure for every progress event.

Regression tests verify initial/retried/edited reads are rejected before opening a missing input and that a source change after one accepted progressive result rejects later results and completion. The focused analysis suite passes 18 tests (`/tmp/clip-audio-source-analysis-tests.log`). This is a partial Audio Studio checkpoint: file-backed playback, export preparation, and other deferred audio tools still need source-context review; do not interpret guarded analysis as proof that all studio actions are covered.

During this checkpoint the user restored the working changes onto newer Office preview and ONNX work. The three stash conflicts were resolved by retaining both dependency sets, both Markdown construction and text-source accessors, the Office PDF preview/cleanup workflow, and checked audio/PDF/model/scene loading. Only the three conflict files were staged to mark them resolved; no commit was made. The integrated workbench library suite passes 1,348 tests with 11 ignored (`/tmp/clip-conflict-resolution-tests.log`), including the Office document viewer and checked audio analysis tests. Formatting and whitespace checks pass. The original architecture baseline now also includes the intervening committed user work in its delta; its reported +25 clone groups and additional complexity findings cannot be attributed to this audio change alone and remain to be separated during the final architecture audit.


## Audio playback and export source checks — 2026-10-20

Audio Studio now prepares its file decoder and initial seek on one cancellable worker path, shared by track playback, slice playback and seek fallback. It retains the search source check around guarded `kernel_interface::open_content` and decoder preparation. Stop, Pause, a superseding request and viewer destruction retire the pending request; an older decoder cannot later install itself. Existing decoder seeks continue to use the held input. The same asynchronous path applies to ordinary Audio Studio playback, removing the repeated synchronous opens. Inline/stem audio views poll this request through their shared analysis tick. HF playback uses its captured analysis; without analysis it waits rather than reopening an unchecked file with an incomplete HF transform.

Save plans and slice-copy plans retain the source check. Export validates before input work and before installation, preserving already installed regions and reporting later failures as partial success. There is no source check after a completed public installation that could erase its reported result. Slice extraction now reuses `audio_export_command`: FFmpeg receives a pinned content input and an owned, seekable staging output; the output-directory handle survives preparation and no-overwrite installation. Cooperative subprocess cancellation remains active. The slice source fingerprint is also checked before private publication.

A native check exposed that Audio Studio did not render its `status_toast` messages in the Files host. The shared audio header now renders active messages, including playback refusal, without mixing SVG icons into notification text. This also exposes existing copy/save status messages in that host. The initial native run `/tmp/clip-audio-studio-source-ui-20261020a/report.json` failed its visible-warning assertion; it must not be counted as a passed UI test. The corrected run `/tmp/clip-audio-studio-source-ui-20261020b/report.json` passes: the selected file actually plays on a private hardware-free audio sink, playback is paused, its parent directory is replaced, and another playback request visibly reports the source change with **zero replacement payload accesses** and no index enrollment. The warning screenshot was visually inspected. Its machine-readable assertions are in `audio-studio-source.json` beside the report.

All 143 audio tests pass after the notification change (`/tmp/clip-audio-source-actions-visible-tests.log`), including source rejection, progressive-analysis checks, cancellation of superseded decoder loads, actual seekable decoder preparation, and partial export success across a source-context change. The existing Space-key playback test now waits for decoder admission and still asserts playback actually starts. The full workbench library suite immediately before the header display change passes **1,351 tests with 11 ignored** (`/tmp/clip-audio-source-actions-library-tests.log`); this is not a new whole-workspace result. The final debug build succeeds (`/tmp/clip-audio-source-actions-visible-build.log`). Formatting, whitespace and acceptance-script syntax checks pass.

Strict library Clippy remains non-green at **271 diagnostics** (`/tmp/clip-audio-source-actions-clippy.log`); reported audio locations are existing nested-condition, signature and tuple-complexity sites. No suppressions were added. Architecture comparison against the historical pre-recovery baseline reports 1,395 clone groups (+24), one fewer than the post-conflict checkpoint, without adding a new excessive-complexity audio function or increasing the maximum complexity/cycle count. The broad regression report still includes intervening committed user work and must be separated during final review. No commits were made; the three earlier conflict resolutions remain staged and there are no unmerged paths. Personal config edits remain unchanged.

The source-action audit is still open for other file-reading Audio Studio tools, notably channel edits, external-editor dispatch and stem-separation preparation. Those require careful handling of owned derived inputs and undo so a new derivative neither inherits obsolete original-source authority nor loses validation on undo. Retrieval quality/precision, large-directory/end-to-end performance, broader Q/A coverage and final integration/packaging remain required. Inspection also reconfirmed that the search directory helper currently serializes one inventory with a 100,000-entry collection bound and a 24 MiB reply cap; large flat-directory behavior needs measurement and an explicit complete/truncated contract rather than assuming the retrieval benchmark covers crawling.


## Large flat-directory inventory transport — 2026-10-20

Measured the actual disposable reader against 100,000 real empty files before changing its transport: the complete inventory contained 100,000 entries with zero skipped entries, took 3.620 seconds to probe/list, and serialized to **53,879,428 bytes**. The parent previously accepted only 24 MiB of stdout, so this otherwise valid inventory could not reach either explicit traversal or index crawling. The owned measurement fixture was removed after measurement; the report is `/tmp/clip-search-inventory-measure.json`. This is evidence of a production crawl gap, not a retrieval-only benchmark.

Directory requests now reuse the existing inherited-handle command mechanism to transfer the inventory into an anonymous owned temporary file. The helper retains all existing root/mount/session and exclusion checks and returns a small typed completion acknowledgement on stdout. The parent parses directly from a buffered descriptor reader rather than holding an additional full stdout payload. Request cancellation and child timeout/reaping still use `CommandRequest`; the anonymous descriptor is released on failed, cancelled and successful calls. Cancellation is checked before and after parent-side inventory parsing. There is still no claim of an atomic filesystem-wide snapshot.

The transfer has an explicit **128 MiB** bound enforced during writes and checked before parent decoding. The helper accepts only an empty regular output descriptor at offset zero, excludes standard I/O descriptors and never resolves an output pathname. Partially written or failed transfers do not publish an inventory. This is an internal transport change, not a new persistent cache or database schema. Other helper replies retain their 24 MiB stdout limit. The existing 100,000-entry-per-directory inventory budget and traversal budget remain explicit: this change makes an otherwise admissible 100,000-file directory work; it does not claim unlimited flat-directory enumeration. The indexer still refuses incomplete inventories and preserves existing rows rather than treating truncation as absence.

New regressions cover an above-24-MiB 100,000-entry serialization round trip, write-budget failure, rejection of standard I/O/nonempty output and preservation of existing supplied content. Reader tests pass **22 with one environment-specific test ignored** (`/tmp/clip-search-inventory-tests.log`). All file-manager library tests pass **683 with three ignored** (`/tmp/clip-search-inventory-file-manager-tests.log`). The debug build succeeds (`/tmp/clip-search-inventory-build.log`), and formatting, whitespace and acceptance-script syntax checks pass.

The private light native run `/tmp/clip-search-inventory-ui-20261020a/report.json` passes against another actual 100,000-file flat directory. Its direct production-helper transfer contains **54,296,672 bytes**, takes **3.869 seconds**, returns **25 stdout bytes**, and preserves all 100,000 distinct paths with zero skipped entries (`inventory-transfer.json`). The actual application then finds the final numbered file through explicit recursive filename traversal, reports zero unsupported/incomplete reads and creates no embedding index (`large-directory-search.json`). Its screenshot was visually inspected. Submit-to-observed time is **9.846 seconds**, including full traversal, rendering, private screenshot capture and OCR; it is not an indexed warm-query or model-inference latency measurement. After successful runner shutdown, the script removes its owned large fixture while retaining reports/screenshots.

Architecture comparison retains the previous 1,395 clone groups (+24 versus the historical pre-recovery baseline) without adding an excessive-complexity inventory function or increasing maximum complexity. The broad baseline regression report remains affected by intervening committed work described above. No commits or additional staging were performed. The full goal remains open: indexed retrieval latency/quality and model-precision comparisons, larger Q/A coverage, remaining source-action checks, final integration and packaging still need completion.


## Retrieval filename prefilter and allocation reduction — 2026-10-20

The SQLite retrieval scan now borrows path BLOB bytes for each row and borrows ordinary display names through shared `filesystem::display_path_cow`. Existing `display_path` keeps its owned-string API, and invalid UTF-8, backslashes and control characters still use the same escaped representation. Already lowercase ASCII filename matching also borrows its input; Unicode and uppercase folding retain the existing implementation. The f16 decoder now has a fixed-array form shared with its original Vec-returning API, so exact similarity scoring no longer allocates a vector per row. The numerical conversion, normalization, accumulation and corruption checks remain unchanged; this is not a reduced-precision or approximate search mode.

The allocation-only optimized run (`/tmp/clip-retrieval-borrow-150k.json`) did not materially improve hybrid timing: exact/rare/common lexical p95 were 124.818/223.546/481.107 ms and default/broad hybrid p95 were 821.564/879.216 ms. These measurements motivated the next change rather than being presented as a performance success. The main lexical cost was checking root memberships for rows that Rust would subsequently reject by filename.

Filename rejection now runs inside SQLite before those membership lookups, through a direct-only deterministic scalar function that calls the same shared escaped/Unicode-aware `name_rank` matcher. Matching rows still pass the complete original source-session, root availability, scope, hidden-file, exclusion and metadata filters. This is a rejection optimization, not an alternative authorization path. It enables rusqlite's existing `functions` feature; no new dependency or schema version is introduced. Ranking/segment fusion, natural tie-breaking and semantic candidate admission are unchanged.

The final optimized component report is `/tmp/clip-retrieval-prefilter-final-150k.json`: **100,000 real fixture files, 150,000 synthetic 768-dimensional f16 vectors, 21 warm samples per case**. P95 is **52.387 ms exact filename**, **51.465 ms rare keyword**, **426.522 ms common keyword**, **815.919 ms default-floor hybrid**, and **867.064 ms broad-floor hybrid**. Exact and rare lexical cases meet the current 100 ms component budget. Common lexical and both hybrid cases still miss their respective 100/500 ms budgets and remain open; no end-to-end acceptance is claimed. A preliminary prefilter report (`/tmp/clip-retrieval-prefilter-150k.json`) overlapped a lint run and is not the final timing reference. The final repeat ran after that lint process exited. First-query timings are not disk-cold, and all these reports exclude model inference, source verification, explanations and UI publication. Their corpus uses 1,025 sparse synthetic prototypes rather than representative learned-vector quality data.

The benchmark driver previously selected the newest executable named `clip_workbench-*`, which can be the ordinary application rather than a test harness. An attempted pre-change run against an older application artifact was stopped and its owned process group reaped; it produced no accepted benchmark result. The driver now proves the Rust test entry point exists before executing an artifact, rejects an explicitly supplied non-test binary, and skips newer application artifacts during automatic selection. Two Python regressions pass, including a sentinel executable that must never run. The actual release harness was built with Cargo's test-artifact metadata and verified by the hardened driver. The final report records its binary hash and source hashes, now including the shared filename formatter and type-navigation matcher.

All **683 file-manager tests pass with three ignored** after the prefilter (`/tmp/clip-retrieval-prefilter-tests.log`). The final focused retrieval tests pass **23 with one benchmark ignored**, including a new SQLite-boundary regression for uppercase, Unicode, invalid UTF-8, backslashes and control-character filenames (`/tmp/clip-retrieval-prefilter-focused-tests.log`). The earlier allocation-only file-manager run also passes 683/3. Optimized test-harness builds succeed (`/tmp/clip-retrieval-prefilter-release-build.log`); formatting, whitespace and Python syntax checks pass. Strict library Clippy remains non-green at **271 diagnostics** (`/tmp/clip-retrieval-prefilter-clippy.log`), without a new diagnostic in the modified retrieval/matcher/formatter/vector-decoder paths. Architecture comparison retains 1,395 clone groups and does not add a new excessive-complexity retrieval function; the broad baseline regression remains affected by intervening committed work described above. No commits or additional staging were performed, no unmerged paths remain, and personal config changes are preserved.

Remaining gates still include common/hybrid retrieval performance, representative semantic quality and model/storage precision comparisons, broader Q/A evidence, remaining source-action checks, and final integration/packaging. The lexical improvement does not complete Phase 6 or prove those other gates.


## Synthetic semantic quality and single-frame video fix — 2026-10-20

Added a reproducible quality component around the actual disposable source reader and pinned CUDA embedding worker. The frozen `scripts/search_model/retrieval_cases.json` contains 60 positive queries (12 each for text, PDF, image, spoken audio and video), 20 no-match queries and 10 exact filename definitions. Files have opaque names. It includes a brief relevant passage in a longer text, a relevant sixteenth PDF page and a clock appearing only in the final five seconds of a 65-second video. The 24 drawn objects were reviewed in `/tmp/clip-semantic-quality-contact.png`; fixtures use no user content or external downloads. Speech is locally synthesized, and visual fixtures are simple drawings, so these results do not establish broad real-world quality. This is semantic audio-content testing, not the excluded audio-sample similarity feature.

The initial run `/tmp/clip-semantic-quality-20261020a.log` failed at the first one-frame video after completing earlier media. It exposed an actual source-reader bug: FFmpeg could successfully seek to a nominal midpoint beyond the clip's only available frame and emit no image. The reader now retries that successful-but-empty decode once, at most five seconds earlier, under the existing guarded decoder/protocol/time limits. It records the actual decoded `showinfo` timestamp, never the requested midpoint. Failed/truncated decoder commands do not trigger this fallback. Normal successful samples retain their existing behavior. The regression verifies one-frame clips with both zero and nonzero container origins, and the source location is correctly zero relative to the container. Five focused video tests pass (`/tmp/clip-quality-single-frame-tests.log`), including existing stream timestamp/audio and network-playlist protections.

The final component run `/tmp/clip-semantic-quality-20261020c/report.json` completes 88 segments and all frozen queries. At the current default 0.25 floor it finds all 60 targets in the top 10, equally for float32 and float16 storage, but **all 20 negative queries also receive results**. This is a concrete cutoff-quality failure, not evidence of release readiness. The preceding `b` report had the same positive/negative outcome; its clock-location assertion incorrectly expected the requested midpoint string `62`, while the actual available frame was at 63 seconds. Ground truth was corrected to the known content window 60–65 seconds, preserving the source, query and expected file. The final report's relevant PDF/video locations pass. This is an assertion correction consistent with the actual-timestamp contract, not a change to the target content.

Exploratory per-modality midpoint candidates, learned from the calibration subset and rounded to 0.01, are text 0.72, image 0.68 and audio 0.64. They retain 100% positive Recall@10 and reduce negative matches to 10% on the initial set, with equal float32/float16 results. However, an additional frozen short-query/independent-negative set (`retrieval_validation.json`) reveals a miss for the shorter window-invoice query: candidate Recall@10 is 11/12 overall, and the small PDF subset is only 1/2. Its negative-match rate is zero. **Application defaults have not been changed.** The original hold-out was inspected during exploration; the report explicitly discloses that history rather than treating it as an untouched final test. Additional validation was frozen before checking these candidate cutoffs. Neither fixture set was weakened to hide a miss.

The component uses production extraction/tokenizer/framing/media preparation but scores maximum semantic similarity per file, not the full SQLite/lexical/RRF pipeline. Float16 conversion follows store encoding/normalization, while both reference scores use common float64 dot accumulation rather than Rust's exact float32 accumulation order. It therefore does not prove the complete storage-precision acceptance gate, Q8-versus-BF16 model equivalence, exact filename tiers, coordinator freshness or UI publication. Its 10-second elapsed time is not an end-to-end performance claim. Reports preserve those limitations and mark full Phase 6 acceptance false. A next concrete task is to run these frozen outputs through production retrieval/fusion before deciding how defaults should change: lexical matching can rescue the short invoice query, while the broad OR fallback may itself add unrelated results. That interaction must be measured rather than assumed.

All file-manager library tests pass **685 with three ignored** (`/tmp/clip-quality-file-manager-tests.log`), and all **27 model-helper Python tests pass** (`/tmp/clip-semantic-quality-python-tests.log`). The current debug Files build succeeds (`/tmp/clip-quality-single-frame-build.log`); formatting, whitespace and Python syntax checks pass. Strict Clippy was not rerun for this checkpoint; its preceding 271-diagnostic failure remains unresolved. Architecture comparison retains 1,395 clone groups (+24 against the historical baseline), adds no listed excessive-complexity quality function and does not increase maximum complexity. The broader baseline report remains a regression for previously documented/intervening work. No commits or staging were added. All model/evaluation processes for these runs have exited and their owned workers were reaped. The goal remains active, including full quality/precision checks, performance, broader Q/A, remaining source actions and final integration/packaging.


## Production rank-fusion quality and provisional cutoffs — 2026-10-20

Added an explicit production-ranking stage over the frozen real-model inputs: it creates an owned temporary SQLite store through the existing enrollment/observation/publication APIs, then runs the actual hard filters, f16 encoder/scorer, filename/FTS component and rank fusion. It does not touch an application database or configure persistent user roots. `scripts/search_quality_ranking.py` reuses the verified test-harness selection and owned process runner; `--require-targets` checks both implicit defaults and candidate overrides. Reports count negative queries with **any** returned result as well as semantic-only results, so unrelated keyword hits cannot hide behind the narrower semantic-only metric. Model/preparation snapshots retain exact extracted passage text for honest FTS evaluation.

The first production ranking report (`/tmp/clip-quality-ranking-baseline.json`) contradicted the earlier semantic-only success: only **25/60** full-description targets remained within the top ten under the 0.25 floors, and candidate cutoffs alone did not repair the problem. Broad OR fallback let incidental words in long descriptions populate a heavily weighted lexical list, crowding out image/audio/video matches. In that run candidate semantic-only noise was low, but almost all negative queries still returned unrelated keyword hits. This is why the component-only positive result was not accepted as full retrieval evidence.

Changed the plan's unconditional no-match OR fallback: embedding descriptions now retain the AND keyword pass and use OR fallback only for short keyword-style payloads of **at most three tokens**. Lexical-only retrieval keeps its earlier behavior. This preserves examples such as `window invoice` and `2019 tax document`, while long paraphrases rely on embeddings instead of incidental-word matches. Hard constraints, exact-name tiers, natural tie-breaking, per-file maximum segment scoring and RRF weights are unchanged. Two regressions cover lexical crowding and short-keyword recovery. An initial short-keyword test failed because its fixture published no segments at all; both new fixtures now publish real below-floor vector/text segments so they exercise FTS rather than an empty table. Synthetic ranking/admission fixtures retain an explicit 0.25 test override, while a separate regression verifies the actual new defaults and user overrides. No production guard assertion was removed.

Adopted **provisional** default floors text **0.72**, image **0.68**, audio **0.64**, shared through `FileSearchConfig::default_semantic_floor` by retrieval, preferences and persistence. Explicit user values—including 0.25—are retained. Changing thresholds does not alter pipeline identity or regenerate embeddings. The public helper documents its unsupported-modality fallback without enabling that modality. The root list remains empty by default. The semantic plan now records these measured decisions and links this ledger. These cutoffs are empirical filters, not probabilities or universal relevance guarantees.

The final report `/tmp/clip-quality-ranking-final.json` uses `/tmp/clip-semantic-quality-20261020e` and passes **72/72 positive queries within top ten**, 100% in every fixture kind, and returns results for **2/32 negative queries (6.25%)** under both actual implicit defaults and candidate overrides. Calibration, exploratory held-out and additional short-query/negative splits are retained separately. All **10 exact filenames rank first against real query embeddings and semantic competitors**. The exact definitions gained stable identifiers in fixture metadata version 2; their strings, target filenames and other ground truth did not change. Earlier ranking snapshots used empty vectors for those ten cases and proved only the lexical path; the final evaluator now rejects every missing query vector, and the final snapshot contains all 114 query embeddings. The old 0.25 profile, still shown separately, returns results for every negative query. This is a synthetic production-component gate, not a broad public benchmark or a BF16 comparison.

Private native runs pass in both themes: dark `/tmp/clip-quality-native-ui-20261020a/report.json` and final light `/tmp/clip-quality-native-ui-20261020b/report.json`. They start without an index, explicitly enroll the generated folder through the Index button, and let the real coordinator generate/publish embeddings. Both finish with **60 ready files, 88 vectors (40 text, 36 image, 12 audio), no failed files**, and declared sampled video coverage. Queries find the image, spoken announcement and the clock visible only late in the video; the retained explanation reports the actual 63-second frame. A short invoice query is recovered by keywords, an unrelated query returns no matches, and the exact filename appears in its expected tier. Screenshots were visually inspected, including the green Index state and decoded frame location. The zero-result view was also corrected to say **No matching files** in the list/footer instead of claiming the nonempty source folder was empty. The final light rerun asserts and visibly shows that distinction.

Validation: final file-manager tests pass **688 with four explicitly ignored** (`/tmp/clip-quality-final-file-manager-tests.log`); core config tests pass **11** (`/tmp/clip-quality-cutoff-core-tests.log`); core doctests pass **4** (`/tmp/clip-quality-core-doctests.log`). Core documentation builds and the rendered helper contract was checked (`/tmp/clip-quality-core-doc-build.log`). All **27 model-helper Python tests plus two harness-selection tests** pass (`/tmp/clip-quality-final-python-tests.log`). Focused retrieval tests previously pass 25/2, and the explicit real-model ranking fixture passes after requiring all query vectors. Debug Files builds pass, including the final no-result UI adjustment (`/tmp/clip-quality-empty-results-build.log`). Formatting, whitespace and script syntax checks pass. Strict library Clippy remains non-green at **271 diagnostics** (`/tmp/clip-quality-fusion-final-clippy.log`); no suppressions were added. Architecture comparison retains 1,395 clone groups (+24 against the historical baseline), with no newly listed excessive-complexity ranking/default function or maximum-complexity increase. The broad regression report remains open for final attribution/review.

No commits or extra staging were performed; earlier conflict resolutions remain staged and there are no unmerged paths. The original personal config edits remain intact. The goal is not complete: representative real-world relevance and Q8/BF16 comparison, a fully matched f32/f16 production comparison, unresolved large-index latency, broader generic/large/visual Q/A coverage, remaining source-action checks, final workspace/integration checks and packaging still require work. This quality checkpoint does not substitute for those requirements.

## Bounded hierarchical text Q/A — 2026-10-21

Replaced the former 24 KiB whole-document answer cutoff with bounded exact-quotation selection and reconciliation using the existing Qwen3.5-9B answer slot. Source extraction now admits up to 512 KiB/512 sources. Small inputs retain the direct-answer path; larger inputs use overlapping 8 KiB windows (256-byte overlap), at most 64 windows, four quotation slots per window, 512 bytes per quote, 64 retained quotes and 24 KiB retained evidence. A saturated four-slot selection is conservatively incomplete. Evidence must occur verbatim in its current source; citations retain their original page/location through renumbering. The inference phase has a 180-second overall budget and 90-second per-call ceiling, with cancellation between calls and the existing worker cancellation during each call. Root/source verification after answering remains in place. No additional model, runtime rebuild, index enrollment or persistent answer storage is introduced.

Reconciliation explicitly distinguishes directly supported local facts from global claims. Selected quotes alone cannot establish whole-file absence, exhaustive totals or non-applicability; those return Unknown rather than a fabricated value or dash. Thus large unrelated documents still need a stronger applicability/global-answer strategy. Visual Q/A also remains unfinished. Incomplete extraction, excessive windows or saturated evidence abstain. Native context/output-budget exhaustion now returns a structured abstention without accepting partial JSON or retiring an otherwise healthy model; actual decoder/grammar failures still fail. Prompt version `generic-qa-v3` invalidates earlier ephemeral answer results.

Validation: nine Rust answer tests pass (`/tmp/clip-answer-context-final-20261021.log`), including late evidence, Unicode boundaries, cancellation, saturated selection and fabricated citations. Twelve Python answer-contract tests pass (`/tmp/clip-answer-python-tests-20261021.log`), including native budget abstention and preserved decoder failures. All 27 ordinary synthetic answer-model cases pass on CUDA with the 9B model (`/tmp/clip-answer-v3-smoke-20261021.json`). The separate evidence-protocol checks previously passed six cases, including conflicting values, explicit false and instruction injection (`/tmp/clip-answer-evidence-model-20261020a/report.json`). The new private native run passes (`/tmp/clip-answer-large-native-20261021a/report.json`): the session column visibly displays **18 points** from after 24 KiB in a 30.3 KiB synthetic document; the Index button remains red and no database is created. The screenshot was visually inspected. These are synthetic checks, not general long-document accuracy evidence.

The broader file-manager run reports **692 passed, one failed, four ignored** (`/tmp/clip-answer-context-file-manager-20261021.log`). The failure is the existing system shutdown test attempting optional-helper admission while the shared budget is occupied; its isolated rerun passes (`/tmp/clip-answer-context-shutdown-rerun-20261021.log`). Do not describe this parallel suite as green. Debug Files builds pass (`/tmp/clip-answer-large-native-build-20261021.log`). Architecture comparison retains 1,395 clone groups (+24 against the historical baseline), after sharing acceptance-model provisioning rather than duplicating it. It additionally flags the bounded orchestration and Python validation/generation functions for complexity; these need review/simplification before final acceptance. Overall maximum complexity and dependency cycle counts remain unchanged. No commits were made. Remaining work still includes global/visual Q/A, quality/reference comparisons, latency, remaining Audio Studio source-action checks and final integration/packaging.

Follow-up simplification separates direct versus windowed Rust orchestration and separates Python coverage/evidence validation from generation, retaining the existing evidence and typed-value validators. All nine Rust answer tests and all 31 model-helper Python tests pass after this refactor (`/tmp/clip-answer-context-refactor-20261021.log`, `/tmp/clip-answer-all-python-final-20261021.log`). The repeated architecture comparison no longer lists excessive-complexity functions in the new answer context or Python answer worker; clone groups remain 1,395. The broader historical regression report remains open for final attribution. The real-model/native checks above preceded this structural refactor.

The complete file-manager test subset passes when serialized: **693 passed, zero failed, four ignored**, 40.65 seconds (`/tmp/clip-answer-context-file-manager-serial-20261021.log`). This confirms the current source tree's tests while retaining the parallel-admission failure above as a test-isolation issue. All model helpers and private acceptance processes from this checkpoint have exited. The full Phase 6 objective remains active and incomplete.

## Still-image typed Q/A with the existing answer model — 2026-10-21

Added a bounded visual answer path for ordinary still images, using the same Qwen3.5-9B Q8_0 answer weights and one lazy vision projection rather than a third model or inference server. The f16 projection is pinned at the existing model repository revision `182be2fd6c7bc44887d88a91cb03ff009cc9f549`: `mmproj-Qwen_Qwen3.5-9B-f16.gguf`, 918,165,952 bytes, SHA-256 `97f420245a85ce129bb764e86a5e21e27d782fe6d6056c6839b9c5fdb8f38289`. Repository API metadata was checked at that revision before acquisition. Explicit Install answer model now acquires the matching projection too; existing text-only installations remain usable without it. The runtime rebuild retains CPU, CUDA and Vulkan backends. This checkpoint tests CUDA vision, not Vulkan/CPU vision throughput or memory ceilings.

The existing root/session/entry-validated disposable reader prepares the image; the answer worker receives bounded PNG bytes rather than user source paths. The C shim decodes directly from that buffer through the existing mtmd helper, with no new Python runtime package. PNG input is capped at 8 MiB and 2048 pixels per side, and the current reader prepares at most 1024 pixels per side. Vision uses at most 1024 image tokens inside the existing 8192-token context/768-token output limits. Application-owned prompt framing is tokenized separately from ordinary question data; image instructions stay untrusted content. Projection weights load only for an explicit visual request and retire with the existing answer slot. Post-answer source verification remains shared with text answers. Questions and results remain session-only and do not enroll folders or create an index.

Visual evidence has a distinct protocol: source 0, a normalized integer region `[left,top,right,bottom]` in 0..1000, and a bounded observation. Python and Rust reject invalid regions/sources and independently validate state, applicability, exact decimal/date/boolean values. The shared type validator is reused, but visual observations are labeled **model observation** in the tooltip instead of being claimed as verified source quotations. Region validation is not factual/field/OCR verification. The model can still select a wrong field or misread pixels; broad visual quality acceptance is open. Prompt/worker identity is now `generic-qa-v4`, including the visual helper's source hash so ephemeral caches retire after changes.

An initial native run skipped image rows under the old text-only queue rule; still images now enter that queue, with an explicit regression retaining the audio exclusion. A second native run revealed that normal sibling imports do not work under production's Python `-I`; helpers now load from explicit bundled paths without modifying the import search path. The shared model-check worker and ordinary answer smoke now also use `-I` to cover that production constraint. Failed native reports `...20261021a` and `...20261021b` remain for review.

The dark run additionally captures the region and **model observation** tooltip. Screenshots were visually inspected. ## Bounded short/scanned/mixed PDF answers — 2026-10-21

Unified answer-source collection for text, ordinary images and PDF pages instead of maintaining separate service implementations. The guarded reader still owns all content access; the answer worker receives only prepared content. A document admits up to 512 source/page parts, 512 KiB extracted text, 16 prepared images and 16 MiB encoded image data. The 90-second source admission deadline is checked between helper calls; active helpers retain their own bounded lifetime. Incomplete extraction or exceeded collection limits returns Unknown without inference. Explicit name-only extractor failures preserve Unsupported and their reason. Existing root/session/file verification after answering remains shared, and no question creates an index or persistent answer data.

A single visual page uses full-image answering. For multiple pages, the same 9B model selects bounded relevant region observations (`visual_evidence`), ordinary text pages use the existing exact-quotation windows, and one shared reducer reconciles all candidates with original page locations/provenance. Visual observations enter reconciliation directly. Shared evidence/context/output limits and a 180-second inference deadline apply across page selection and reconciliation (model startup retains its separate existing bound). Expired shared deadlines now yield Unknown rather than treating a healthy model as failed. Selection alone still cannot establish whole-file absence, exhaustive aggregates or non-applicability. Prompt version is `generic-qa-v5`; source hashing also retires earlier ephemeral results. No extra model or C runtime change was needed.

The source reader's current sparse-page rule is **fewer than 200 trimmed extracted characters**, not a complete visual-coverage test. Those pages include a raster preview; denser pages continue through extracted text. Image-routed pages use visual observations rather than duplicating their short extracted text as another evidence-selection input. Dense pages are not automatically rendered to answer questions about unextracted diagrams, imagery or layout; this is a remaining coverage limitation. The earlier summary's shorthand “non-whitespace characters” was imprecise relative to the actual reader. This checkpoint does not change semantic index segmentation/revisions or trigger an index rebuild.

## Checked channel edits, owned working inputs and undo provenance — 2026-10-21

Channel transforms now validate their captured read context before opening input, pin the ordinary file and compare its observed FileSource identity, then hand the retained input to FFmpeg through the existing content-descriptor command builder. They revalidate the source before analysis and before admitting the result. `ReadCheck::open` extends the existing source-check mechanism with this shared admission path; the descriptor remains owned during rendering. The shared audio export builder now has explicit export/session modes: exports retain their existing range/codec behavior, while channel sessions process the whole track as float32 WAV without rounded range endpoints. FFmpeg's input protocol whitelist is local file/pipe. This is not a claim of general sandboxing of every legacy audio tool.

The render writes to an owned seekable staging file under a retained ordinary-directory handle, and analysis reads its held descriptor. A read-check closure owns the artifact and its captured FileSource. Deferred operations, active state and undo/redo therefore keep the file and directory alive; the final owner removes the temporary file through its retained directory, including discarded completions. No raw pathname removal is needed for stale channel results. A derivative gets its own identity/read check, so an invalidated original search root does not disable the owned working copy. AudioEditSnapshot now retains the active read context and input FileSource along with its path; undo restores the original observation and redo restores the derivative's observation. ReadCheck equality compares immutable callback identity for snapshot comparisons, without performing I/O on the UI thread.

The existing unsaved-edit check previously considered only parameter edits. It now also detects working-path changes in undo history, so leaving a channel-edited session prompts for explicit discard. Undo back to the unmodified input is clean; redo is dirty again. Export/write lifetime behavior remains separate. Channel job errors now show the actual failure text instead of nested Rust Result debug formatting.

Validation: the 146 audio tests pass serialized (`/tmp/clip-channel-source-checked-tests-20261021.log`). The initial parallel audio run failed two existing scenarios while the serialized run passed; that result is retained (`/tmp/clip-channel-source-tests-20261021.log`) and its precise parallel-interference cause is not proven here. New regressions reject an expired context before opening input, reject replacement of an observed input, preserve derivatives after original-context invalidation, restore guards/identities through undo/redo, retain artifacts for deferred checks, and clean them after the last owner. Final channel tests pass 13, FileSource tests pass seven, and the final close-guard/viewer check passes eight (`/tmp/clip-channel-source-discard-tests-20261021.log`, `/tmp/clip-channel-source-file-source-tests-20261021.log`, `/tmp/clip-channel-source-viewer-tests-20261021.log`).

Native dark acceptance passes (`/tmp/clip-channel-source-native-20261021d/report.json`): selected audio plays through the private sink, root replacement makes both playback and a whole-track channel edit visibly reject the source, and syscall tracing records **zero replacement payload reads**. The stronger light run passes (`/tmp/clip-channel-undo-native-20261021c/report.json`): a successful edit remains playable after root replacement, undo visibly rejects the replacement, redo plays the owned edit, and leaving requires the existing explicit-discard dialog. Audio checks require a fresh 32 KiB recording window with nonzero samples, rather than accepting old sink data. Replacement-payload accounting covers those studio operations before returning to the browser. The test confirms no index is created. Evidence is also in `channel-undo-source.json`; screenshots of the rejection and discard dialog were visually inspected.

Retained native failures were harness preconditions: channel tools initially had Regions selected without a range; a status toast shifted controls when Track was selected after the failure; and light-theme OCR omitted a disabled Swap label. Tests now select Track before causing the source error and observe the channel-specific Apply-to controls. Positive edit completion, source rejection, private audio output and trace assertions remain required.

The full workbench library passed 1,377 tests with 12 ignored (`/tmp/clip-channel-source-library-20261021.log`). A full workspace run passed **2,638 test instances, zero failures, 26 ignored** (`/tmp/clip-channel-source-workspace-20261021.log`), before the final small unsaved-path predicate addition; that final addition is covered by the channel/viewer tests and explicit-discard native run above. Final debug Files build, formatting, whitespace and acceptance-script syntax checks pass. Architecture comparison retains 1,395 clone groups (+24 against the original historical baseline) with no newly flagged excessive-complexity channel/ReadCheck function after admission/artifact preparation were separated. The broad historical regression and existing strict-Clippy diagnostics remain open.

## Guarded asynchronous audio range deletion — 2026-10-21

Range deletion now shares the channel-edit worker and owned working-file lifecycle instead of running synchronous path-based FFmpeg on the UI thread. Both actions use ReadCheck admission, a pinned input, retained output-directory handles, owned seekable staging, source revalidation and worker-side analysis. Session changes reject late completions. Undo/redo retains each working input's identity and read context; the final owner releases its temporary artifact. Closing during this cancellable preparation does not authorize a write to an original file.

Deletion first bakes current track/region effects on the original timeline, then trims and joins surviving ranges. The previous implementation reset those effects without rendering them. Existing complex crossfade graphs and simple gain/effect filters feed the same deletion graph. Float32 working WAVs avoid unnecessary 16-bit intermediate quantization. Boundary cuts, reversed selections and mono inputs are supported; invalid/empty or whole-session removal is rejected. Existing channel edits retain their prior parameter/display behavior. Their shared completion also installs waveform/spectrum data and preserves undo/discard behavior without a second edit pipeline.

All **149 audio tests pass** serialized (`/tmp/clip-session-range-final-tests-20261021.log`), including new surviving-sample gain/crossfade checks, mono handling, boundary/reversed ranges, expired source admission and stale-session completion rejection. The earlier 18-test edit subset passed before correcting its purported mono fixture to actually have one channel; the final run uses the corrected fixture. A first test compile failed on a private-field assertion; that assertion was removed without broadening production visibility.

The private native scenario passes in dark and light themes (`/tmp/clip-range-undo-native-20261021a/report.json`, `/tmp/clip-range-undo-native-20261021b/report.json`). It selects a slice, deletes it, plays the owned derivative after original-root replacement, undoes and visibly rejects replacement playback and deletion, then redoes and plays the derivative again. Syscall tracing records **zero replacement payload accesses** during those operations, no semantic database is created, and returning to the browser requires explicit discard. The derived waveform/duration and deletion status were visually inspected. The acceptance scenario reuses the channel undo/redo fixture and private sink checks.

Debug Files builds, formatting, whitespace and acceptance Python syntax checks pass. Architecture comparison has 1,394 clone groups, one fewer than the preceding channel checkpoint (still +23 against the historical baseline); no new range/session-edit function appears in its excessive-complexity findings. Overall historical regression and existing strict-Clippy diagnostics remain unresolved. The semantic plan's obsolete name-only source-code paragraph was reconciled with the existing bounded plain-text extraction implementation and its regression test.

This checkpoint completes range deletion, not Phase 6. Stem preparation/external-editor source handoffs, broader quality/reference and latency checks, packaging and the final requirement-by-requirement audit remain open. No commits or staging were added; personal config changes remain intact.

## Guarded background audio-editor handoff — 2026-10-21

Audio Studio's editor action no longer renders synchronously or launches the original source pathname. It captures the current session filter, identity and ReadCheck, prepares a float32 WAV on the existing bounded audio worker, rechecks the source context and observed identity, and hands a verified materialized copy to the shared EscapeHatchLauncher. Complex crossfade graphs are supported. The status identifies that a session copy was opened. The UI remains responsive during preparation, repeated clicks cannot submit a second pending job, and dropping the viewer cancels preparation without terminating an already accepted external application.

The file-only rendering stage was extracted from the existing channel/range pipeline into render_session_file. It retains pinned input and output-directory handles, writes through an owned seekable staging descriptor, checks the source again, and syncs the output. Channel/range edits then do their existing analysis/owned-artifact installation; editor handoffs avoid unnecessary waveform/spectrum analysis. Failed rendering, source admission, cancellation or launch leaves the temporary copy drop-cleaned. Successful launches disable cleanup on that copy: a launcher may forward its pathname and exit before the actual editor opens it, and closing the viewer must not invalidate the handoff. Accepted copies remain private (0600) in the existing temporary audio-session directory. They are not part of the index or a source-file save.

**Behavior/storage tradeoff:** unedited files now also open as a session copy, rather than letting the editor reopen and potentially save over the original source. The current copy is rendered as float32 WAV; this can cost decoding time and disk space for long/compressed/high-rate inputs. Accepted handoffs remain on disk until external/manual/system temporary-file cleanup, rather than being deleted on launcher exit. This intentionally preserves forwarded-editor access and possible unsaved external edits; a bounded retained-copy lifecycle and avoiding unnecessary decode/copy costs remain packaging/performance follow-up. Failed handoffs are cleaned immediately. Arbitrary configured editors and xdg-open are explicitly trusted external applications; they own their process and subsequent filesystem behavior.

All **151 audio tests pass** serialized (`/tmp/clip-audio-editor-tests-20261021.log`). New regressions reject an expired deferred source before payload access, preserve accepted copies after their original is removed, and clean failed launches, cancellation, source replacement after rendering and expired contexts. The crossfade handoff test now exercises the common pinned renderer rather than the removed synchronous pathname helper. An initial compile identified one obsolete channel-test assertion of the old original-path behavior; it was removed because handoffs now intentionally copy.

Native dark and light passes are retained (`/tmp/clip-audio-editor-native-20261021a/report.json`, `/tmp/clip-audio-editor-native-20261021b/report.json`). A recording stand-in editor opens two distinct real WAV copies, verifies private permissions and their survival after the viewer is discarded, while undo to the replaced original visibly rejects the editor action. Tracing records **zero replacement payload accesses** and no semantic database is created. A repeated light run c timed out waiting for the second launch; its failure artifacts remain. The precise cause is not proven. The strengthened final light run explicitly observes the redo frame and requires fresh private-sink audio from the restored derivative before requesting its editor handoff; it passes (`/tmp/clip-audio-editor-native-20261021d/report.json`, `audio-editor-source.json`). Its copy/rejection screenshots were inspected. Shared root replacement/trace-offset fixture setup avoids duplicating this scenario across audio acceptance scripts.

The full workspace passes **2,648 test instances, zero failures, 26 ignored** (`/tmp/clip-audio-editor-workspace-20261021.log`). Debug Files builds, formatting, whitespace and acceptance-script Python syntax checks pass. Architecture comparison retains 1,394 clone groups (+23 against the historical baseline), maximum complexity 195 versus baseline 196, and no new excessive-complexity editor/session-render function. The broader historical regression and previous strict-Clippy findings remain open. A second stale name-only-code statement in the semantic plan's execution defaults was reconciled with the already implemented bounded plain-text source-code reader.

This completes the editor input handoff checkpoint, not Phase 6. Stem preparation still needs its deferred source/pinned-input migration: the current Python wrapper re-execs the venv and starts Demucs via subprocess without descriptor forwarding, so changing only the outer Rust argument would be insufficient. Broader relevance/reference comparisons, large-index latency, retained handoff-copy policy, packaging and the final scope audit remain. Audio sample similarity remains excluded. No commits or staging were added, and the user's personal config diff is unchanged.


## Checked stem preparation and owned outputs — 2026-10-21

Stem preparation captures the session snapshot (including FileSource and ReadCheck) and current filter graph before worker submission. The worker reuses render_session_file to normalize the admitted input to float32 WAV with current effects, then revalidates the source before accepting model outputs. Python receives inherited descriptors for that prepared input and a private per-run output directory through CommandRequest. Completed previews use new_with_metadata_checked with worker-collected metadata, their own output FileSource and a read check that owns the output-directory lifetime. They do not inherit an expired original search-root guard.

The wrapper now uses Demucs' tensor API in the same Python process: sphn decodes the prepared WAV, convert_audio supplies the model channel/rate layout, and separate_tensor runs inference. This replaces the additional Python subprocess that dropped inherited descriptors. Interpreter replacement retains isolated Python mode and the inherited descriptor arguments. The wrapper requires the Demucs 4.1 tensor API and its dependencies; the installed 4.1.0 source was inspected and the unavailable-dependency error identifies the requirement. No new packages or model weights were installed for these checks. This is an implementation change to source handoff, not audio sample similarity or a model-quality claim.

Outputs use only the four fixed relative WAV filenames under the pinned job directory; external/nested manifest paths are rejected. An Arc-owned temporary directory and retained parent/directory handles keep files alive for the preview and deferred readers, then remove the unexported job after the last owner. An accepted explicit multi-stem external launch retains its files for forwarded/daemonized applications, preserving that existing action's input lifetime. Other grouped playback/save/external-action admission still needs its separate audit. The source/worker boundary is isolated from the large existing studio renderer in stem_separation/worker.rs.

Desktop testing found that tempfile's default directory permissions here were **0755**, rather than the assumed private mode. Creation now explicitly requests **0700**, with a Rust regression and native permission assertion. These failed reports remain. The runner uses a tensor stand-in with real sphn/Torch resampling and Demucs WAV writing; it does not load weights or evaluate actual separation quality.

The two Python wrapper checks pass (`/tmp/clip-stem-wrapper-tests-20261021.log`): descriptor input remains the original despite pathname replacement, real mono 8 kHz float WAV becomes four valid stereo 44.1 kHz outputs through the tensor path, and interpreter re-exec preserves isolation/descriptor arguments. ## Guarded asynchronous grouped stem playback and export — 2026-10-21

Grouped playback now prepares/seeks all audible stem decoders on a bounded worker, using the shared checked decoder with each stem's captured FileSource. The complete batch is revalidated before publication; no track starts on a partial failure. A shared pending request is replaced on seek/mute/solo changes and cancelled on pause/stop, leaving the tab or closing the studio. The playback clock starts after decoder publication. ## Guarded multi-stem external-editor preparation — 2026-10-21

The multi-stem editor action now shares the existing cancellable AudioEditorJob and checked session renderer with single-file handoffs. It captures each stem's session snapshot/filter, renders current per-stem effects on a worker, then revalidates the entire batch before launching. The common launch_rendered_handoffs path admits one or several rendered copies and retains all copies only after an accepted launch. Failure, cancellation or stale input drops every prepared copy. This replaces direct UI-thread spawning with the original generated-stem paths.

A private 0700 handoff workspace holds named vocals/drums/bass/other aliases to the independent rendered copies. Audacity retains the isolated profile and LOF behavior; other configured editors receive the named aliases as arguments. Accepted copies/profile survive forwarding or daemonization, independently of the viewer. The launched editor child is reaped without tying its lifetime to the studio. The original stem job is no longer marked permanently retained: its existing ReadCheck ownership is sufficient for deferred readers and normal final-owner cleanup. The redundant per-stem retention field, worker result ownership field and mutable retention mutex were removed.

All **164 audio tests pass** serialized (`/tmp/clip-stem-editor-final-audio-tests-20261021.log`). Coverage includes rendering current gain into independent copies, accepted copies surviving source/viewer release, expired input rejection, an early stem expiring during later preparation, cancelled/failed/changed-source two-copy batch cleanup, and failed-launch/unsafe-name alias cleanup. Existing isolated Audacity configuration, grouped playback, export and undo tests remain in that run. Native/current workspace and architecture verification follow below when terminal. Accepted-copy storage/cleanup policy, semantic reference/quality/performance gates, packaging and the final requirement audit remain unfinished. No commits or staging were added.

Native editor acceptance passes in dark and light themes (`/tmp/clip-stem-editor-native-20261021a/report.json`, `/tmp/clip-stem-editor-native-20261021b/report.json`). The recording editor reads four real independent WAV copies through named aliases, verifies private copies/profile, then the studio rejects a replaced stem with **zero replacement payload reads** and no second launch. Closing the viewer cleans the original stem job while accepted copies and the LOF/profile remain available after the recording editor exits. The fixture then reclaims only its exact reported artifacts. Both themes' success/rejection screenshots were inspected. The tensor fixture performs real WAV/resampling/writer I/O but does not load model weights or establish separation quality. Shared ready_stems setup now serves the source/playback, save and editor acceptance scenarios, retaining their model-run/count/privacy assertions.

Retention policy is now an explicit decision rather than an unresolved cleanup mechanism: accepted external-editor copies/profile remain until external/manual/system temporary-file cleanup. The application cannot infer that a forwarded or daemonized editor has finished, and sweeping these files could delete unsaved external work. Failed/cancelled preparations still clean immediately. Status identifies a session-copy handoff; users save permanent results through the external editor. This trades potentially accumulated disk use after repeated explicit handoffs for preserving edits. The files are private, but normal temporary-file policies can remove them; they are not a permanent-save guarantee. No new cleanup UI or automatic age-based deletion is added to Phase 6.

## Direct normalized-vector scoring experiment (removed) — 2026-10-21

This section records a temporary prototype, removed after the measurements below. In that prototype, stored-vector decoding exposes its existing validated f32 components and f64 norm to both normalization and scoring. Public normalized decoding retains its exact previous component conversion; the scorer instead accumulates a f64 dot product and divides once by the stored norm, avoiding 768 per-component normalization divisions per vector per query. This is algebraically equivalent apart from floating-point accumulation/rounding; storage format/schema, query admission, source filters, floors and rank fusion are unchanged. Existing corruption/zero/nonfinite checks are shared, not bypassed. A deterministic 128-pair numerical regression compares against the normalized f32 reference within 2e-6 and checks self-similarity and malformed vectors.

All **159 search tests pass, three explicitly ignored** (`/tmp/clip-direct-cosine-search-tests-20261021.log`), including the storage decoder's existing bitwise reference checks. The frozen production-ranking run passes (`/tmp/clip-direct-cosine-quality-20261021.json`): **72/72 positive Recall@10**, every modality 100% on these synthetic fixtures, **2/32 negatives returning any result**, and all **10 exact filenames first**. This remains the same limited real-model synthetic corpus, not representative public relevance or a BF16 comparison. The separate release-scale benchmark now obtains its default floor from current FileSearchConfig instead of hardcoding the old 0.25, and records the actual floor in each case. Optimized build, measured timings and architecture comparison follow below when terminal; no performance improvement is claimed from source inspection alone.

The direct-scoring experiment does **not** provide a useful measured improvement: `/tmp/clip-direct-cosine-150k-20261021.json` records 100,000 real fixture files / 150,000 synthetic f16 vectors / 21 warm samples, p95 **44.794 ms exact**, **56.304 ms rare keyword**, **445.760 ms common keyword**, **817.523 ms default hybrid**, **889.958 ms broad hybrid**. Common lexical and hybrid cases still fail 100/500 ms targets. Default-floor synthetic hits are unchanged by correcting 0.25 to 0.72 because the corpus's low prototypes are below both cutoffs. The frozen 104 non-filename query cases also retain identical returned file orders, but numerical compatibility alone does not justify the change.

The production scoring/decoder experiment and its prototype-only numerical test were therefore **removed**. Current store.rs, retrieval.rs and retrieval/quality.rs SHA-256 hashes exactly match the earlier accepted `/tmp/clip-quality-ranking-final.json` source hashes, preserving the established behavior rather than retaining unproven extra precision/abstraction. The earlier paragraphs in this experiment section describe that removed prototype, not current implementation. Only the benchmark default-floor correction remains, plus ignored-benchmark diagnostic probes: independent single warm SQL scans and an in-memory scoring loop after latency sampling. These probes are explicitly not an additive decomposition or end-to-end measurements. The restored implementation passes **158 search tests, three ignored** (`/tmp/clip-retrieval-diagnostics-search-tests-20261021.log`). A fresh optimized diagnostic benchmark follows when terminal.

The restored-source optimized diagnostic report is `/tmp/clip-retrieval-diagnostics-150k-20261021.json` (100,000 owned real fixture files, 150,000 synthetic f16 vectors, 21 warm samples/case). P95: **57.303 ms exact filename**, **46.906 ms rare keyword**, **468.358 ms common keyword**, **889.745 ms default hybrid**, **894.076 ms broad hybrid**. The common lexical/hybrid budgets remain unmet. Variation between these runs is not evidence that the discarded scoring prototype helped; the original implementation remains in place. Reports retain actual default floor 0.72, corpus counts and source/binary hashes; first-query values are not disk-cold and no end-to-end/model latency is included.

Independent single-pass probes after sampling show common-keyword SQL stepping 150,000 rows in **309.485 ms**, semantic SQL stepping 150,000 rows in **324.021/360.323 ms**, and hot in-memory scoring probes of 150,000 vectors in **273.055/268.022 ms** (default/broad cases). These are independent warm probes, not a decomposition to subtract or add into query latency; the scoring prototypes fit a smaller hot working set than the whole index. Both SQL materialization/admission and scoring are substantial. This supports investigating the plan's bounded vector working set and lookup/scoring reuse with source-generation checks, rather than keeping the ineffective arithmetic rewrite. Freshness, hard filters before candidate caps, corruption rejection, memory limits and cancellation remain requirements for that next work. No new cache or approximation is implemented in this checkpoint.

Final diagnostic architecture comparison retains 1,397 clone groups (+26 historical), maximum complexity 195 versus baseline 196, and no newly reported excessive-complexity diagnostic function. Formatting, whitespace and affected Python syntax checks pass. The benchmark process and all Cargo/native/model-evaluation handles for this checkpoint are terminal. No commits/staging were added, and the personal config diff is unchanged. ## Bounded normalized-vector reuse and covering admission — 2026-10-21

Added a Store-owned lazy cache of validated, normalized float32 working vectors over canonical f16 SQLite data. SQL root/session/availability/scope admission and current Rust policy/metadata checks still run on every query; no candidate eligibility or query result is cached. A query-local SQLite callback reports cached IDs so a derived covering segment lookup can avoid reading the vector table on warm rows. A distinct typed marker separates cached vectors from cold NULL/lexical-only segments; NULL rows skip semantic metadata/scoring, preserving prior behavior even with unrelated malformed metadata. Cache hits reuse exactly the existing f32 normalization and summation, without the removed arithmetic experiment's rounding changes.

The packed matrix plus conservatively budgeted hash/allocator overhead is capped at **512 MiB**. It is populated only as eligible vector rows are scored. At capacity or after allocation failure, further rows use the original uncached exact scorer; no scope/candidate window is silently truncated. Generation changes clear/reuse the allocation; growth releases old buffers before reserving replacements, rather than keeping a second matrix. Idle buffers release after five minutes without vector queries, independently of the model keep-loaded option. This deliberately avoids retaining large CPU buffers just because weights were pinned. Cancellation retains only valid derived cache entries and still interrupts SQL/row iteration.

Cache epochs include SQLite connection total_changes and data_version. Every observed database write conservatively invalidates all vectors; this is not incremental cache maintenance and can make the next query cold after even a metadata-only write. Results are rejected if the database changes during retrieval. Restore through SQLite's backup API and explicit Clear invalidate immediately because backup page replacement need not increment ordinary SQL write counters. The new segment_admission index is a derived accelerator recreated on open/restore; canonical schema/version 5, data/backup format and extraction pipeline remain unchanged. No new dependency, filesystem content read, persistent normalized-vector copy, default root or source mutation is introduced.

Current search tests pass **164, three explicitly ignored** (`/tmp/clip-vector-cache-covering-final-search-tests-20261021.log`). Regressions cover exact cached/uncached scores, capacity fallback without scope loss, bounded maximum reservation and one-matrix refresh, corruption rejection, local/external writes, policy changes without database changes, post-read epoch failure, idle release, restore/clear release and filtering before corrupt-vector decoding. The derived-index recreation test covers the new index. Initial compile errors were child-module method visibility, corrected without exposing APIs beyond search internals.

Frozen production ranking passes before and after the covering change (`/tmp/clip-vector-cache-quality-20261021.json`, `/tmp/clip-vector-cache-covering-quality-20261021.json`): 72/72 positive Recall@10, two of 32 negatives returning any result, and all ten exact filenames first. The pre-covering cache report's full top records—including scores and locations—match the earlier accepted baseline for all 104 non-filename cases. This is still the limited synthetic real-model fixture, not a BF16/real-world claim. Evaluation provenance now includes store/vectors.rs hashes.

The initial scale run (`/tmp/clip-vector-cache-150k-20261021.json`) retained **zero cached vectors**: its existing bulk SQL seed bypassed Store::publish and the live Store's vector counter. It tested uncached fallback, not a warm cache. The fixture now reopens the persisted store to reconstruct counters and explicitly requires all 150,000 test vectors to be cached within budget before sampling. The corrected pre-covering run (`/tmp/clip-vector-cache-counted-150k-20261021.json`) retains 150,000 vectors with a **468,205,568-byte conservative reservation bound** (~446.5 MiB). Its p95 default/broad hybrid results are **716.180/778.017 ms**, versus the prior restored baseline's 889.745/894.076 ms. This is progress but still misses 500 ms; common-keyword p95 **457.446 ms** still misses 100 ms. The subsequent covering-index benchmark and native/workspace verification follow below when terminal. No goal-completion claim follows from these component checks.

## Request-scoped streaming search progress — 2026-10-21

Semantic scanning now exposes one shared atomic checked-reference counter through the existing coordinator snapshot. It replaces the count every 2,048 SQL-admitted segment references and publishes the final partial batch; the UI polls at its existing 100 ms pending-search cadence and displays `Searching · N indexed parts checked…`. References include aliases and rows subsequently rejected by Rust policy/metadata, so the display deliberately claims neither a unique file/vector count nor a percentage of a fabricated scope total. The scan continues through its whole scope beyond the 500-result window and the vector cache limit; no progress event queue or cached eligibility is introduced. Cancellation still runs per row and during SQL stepping.

The counter is tagged by request ID. Only the active pending request can paint it. Accepting a replacement search/answer retires the old counter, while failed queue admission leaves existing state intact. A retired worker may still hold/update its own Arc without changing the current display. Search completion, including failure/cancellation, removes the snapshot progress. Source verification and result publication retain their prior guards. No new model, dependency, source read, index root or persistent data is added.

The first progress regression checkpoint passes **168 search tests, zero failures, three ignored** (`/tmp/clip-scan-progress-ui-search-tests-20261021.log`): a 4,500-reference scan checks complete scope and final partial-batch publication, an empty scope resets the count, pre-cancelled work is rejected, request mismatch/retirement and failed queue admission are covered, and rendered egui text is present only for a matching pending request. The final text uses `indexed parts` instead of the prototype's more technical `indexed segment references`; the full workspace and optimized benchmark with active progress follow when terminal. The scale benchmark now uses the real atomic counter for semantic queries and asserts its final count, rather than timing a disabled progress path.

The final `indexed parts` wording and enabled scale counter pass the serialized full workspace: **2,710 passed, zero failures, 26 ignored across 26 suites** (`/tmp/clip-scan-progress-workspace-20261021.log`). A subsequent equivalent cleanup uses the project's existing integer is_multiple_of spelling for the 2,048-reference cadence, avoiding a new manual-is-multiple-of lint. The optimized affected tests and scale benchmark verify that spelling when terminal below; no broader behavior changed in that cleanup. The rendered UI test covers the actual progress label, not just the snapshot getter.

The equivalent cadence cleanup passes **168 optimized search tests, zero failures, three ignored** (`/tmp/clip-scan-progress-release-search-tests-20261021.log`). The current optimized progress-enabled scale run (`/tmp/clip-scan-progress-150k-20261021.json`, original synthetic fixture) reports p95 **39.572 ms exact filename, 36.992 ms rare keyword, 387.159 ms common keyword, 402.179 ms default hybrid and 451.348 ms broad hybrid**. Both hybrid cases check all **150,000 references**. Frozen production ranking also passes (`/tmp/clip-scan-progress-quality-20261021.json`) with unchanged full top records for all 104 non-filename cases; current defaults retain 72/72 positives, 2/32 negatives with any result and all ten exact filenames first. ## Matched production float16/float32 storage comparison — 2026-10-21

The frozen quality harness now includes a test-only float32 reference through the existing normalized-vector cache. Each original model-output vector receives float32 encode and decode L2 normalization, omitting half conversion; the reference then uses the exact production SQLite scope/source/metadata admission, f32 accumulation, cosine floors, lexical matching and rank fusion. The canonical SQLite f16 blobs, schema/pipeline and application binary behavior are unchanged. The oracle is compiled only under cfg(test), cannot be requested through the application worker, covers every stored segment through unambiguous file/modality/location IDs, and fits the existing cache budget. It is a working-representation oracle, not a new persistent f32 database format.

The existing Python-only comparison used common float64 dot accumulation and therefore could not prove the Rust storage/scoring gate. The production report now retains full f16 and f32 cases, exact-name checks, changed top-ten orders, expected-file rank changes and matched returned-segment score differences. The Python gate enforces at most one percentage point Recall@10 loss and exact-name correctness for the reference. Missing reference data from an older test executable fails --require-targets with an explicit rebuild reason. Reports hash vectors.json, the frozen manifest/component report, validation cases, driver, binary and relevant Rust sources, and retain the frozen model pipeline metadata. These provenance additions do not turn synthetic fixtures into representative real-world quality evidence.

Debug search verification passes **169 tests, zero failures, three ignored** (`/tmp/clip-matched-precision-search-tests-20261021.log`). An oracle unit test proves that f32 results differ from half quantization and rejects wrong dimensions, nonfinite/zero vectors and inputs exceeding the prepared reference capacity. Three Python gate tests cover the one-percentage-point boundary, top-ten recall, order/score changes without recall loss, and mismatched query sets. The first matched frozen run (`/tmp/clip-matched-storage-precision-20261021.json`) passes with **72/72 positives in both representations, 2/32 negatives returning results in both, zero changed top-ten file orders or expected-file ranks**, and all ten exact names first. Across 91 matched returned-segment score occurrences, the maximum absolute difference is **0.00002473592758178711**. The optimized verification follows below when terminal; this is not a Q8/BF16 model-weight comparison.

Optimized search verification also passes **169 tests, zero failures, three ignored** (`/tmp/clip-matched-precision-release-tests-20261021.log`). The final optimized frozen report is `/tmp/clip-matched-storage-precision-release-final-20261021.json`. It retains the same result: zero Recall@10 loss, no changed top-ten file orders or expected-file ranks across 104 matched search cases, all ten exact names first in both representations, and maximum returned-segment difference **2.473592758178711e-5** across 91 matched score occurrences. Original production f16 top records remain identical to `/tmp/clip-quality-ranking-final.json` across those 104 cases. The final gate additionally verifies matching query text, split, modality kind and expected file, so reusing IDs with changed expectations cannot pass. The three Python gate tests pass, including that mismatch. Report provenance binds the actual prepared vector file, manifest/component report, validation fixture, comparison driver, Rust sources, test executable and frozen CUDA/3090 model pipeline metadata.

Final architecture comparison retains **1,410 clone groups**, maximum complexity **195** and the same broad historical warnings; this test-only oracle/comparison adds no clone group or reported excessive-complexity function. Formatting, whitespace and affected Python syntax checks pass. No new runtime code path, user index, model installation, personal config change, staging or commit is introduced.
